People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came in because I just… I can’t seem to get out from under this heaviness. It’s like this hopeless feeling that sits on me all day, and I keep thinking about everything I worked so hard to rebuild after the divorce, and it feels like it’s all collapsed. Like I’m just sitting underneath it, looking at the pieces, and I don’t know how to move or what I’m supposed to do next.

I know that sounds dramatic, maybe, but it doesn’t feel like a bad day or a rough week. It feels bigger than that. Heavy. Kind of like there’s no air in the room, even when everything around me is technically still there. I just feel like I can’t shake it.

Not exactly right after the divorce itself, no. The divorce was… obviously it was a lot, and there was plenty of stress around it, but this particular heaviness really settled in about a month ago.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just… I can’t seem to get out from under this heaviness. Like I’m just sitting underneath it, looking at the pieces, and I don’t know how to move or what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, maybe, but it doesn’t feel like a bad day or a rough week. It feels bigger than that. Heavy. Kind of like there’s no air in the room, even when everything around me is technically still there. I just feel like I can’t shake it.\n\nDoctor: Did this heaviness start after the divorce?\n\nPatient: Not exactly right after the divorce itself, no. Yes. It’s been… it’s been more than just feeling bad and still getting through things. I’m not functioning the way I normally do.

At work, it got really obvious. I’m usually careful — I mean, my whole job depends on being careful and thinking through consequences — but I started making questionable calls, not like intentionally reckless, but I just wasn’t tracking things the way I should. I’d sit in meetings and kind of fall apart, either getting overwhelmed or not being able to explain myself clearly, and I could tell people were noticing. My supervisor actually had to take me off active project decisions for now, which was humiliating, but also probably necessary.

And at home it’s not much better. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like the normal things you do to keep your life from completely unraveling. Everything just piles up and I look at it and can’t seem to start.

That’s when it went from feeling stressed or overwhelmed to this constant cloud over everything. It’s been hanging over me for about a month now, pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Does this heaviness feel tied specifically to the financial problems after the divorce?\n\nPatient: It’s tied to it, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only that, if that makes sense. The money problems after the divorce were like… the thing that made me look around and realize how much had fallen apart. But the feeling itself is bigger than just bills or numbers.\n\nIt’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I can’t shake, like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has collapsed anyway. Like I did all this work to get some kind of stability again, and now I’m just sitting under the wreckage of it. So yes, the financial stuff is part of it, definitely, but it feels more like it opened up this whole sense that everything is ruined and I don’t know how to get out from under it.\n\nDoctor: Has this heavy, hopeless feeling been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been there pretty much every day. I mean, I wish I could say it comes and goes in a normal way, like I have a bad afternoon and then I reset, but it hasn’t felt like that.\n\nIt’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I just can’t shake, like everything I built back up after the divorce has collapsed and I’m just sitting under it. Even when I’m trying to do regular things or tell myself to keep moving, it’s still there in the background, like a weight pressing on everything. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been… it’s been more than just feeling bad and still getting through things. I’m not functioning the way I normally do.\n\nAt work, it got really obvious. I’m usually careful — I mean, my whole job depends on being careful and thinking through consequences — but I started making questionable calls, not like intentionally reckless, but I just wasn’t tracking things the way I should. I’d sit in meetings and kind of fall apart, either getting overwhelmed or not being able to explain myself clearly, and I could tell people were noticing. My supervisor actually had to take me off active project decisions for now, which was humiliating, but also probably necessary.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like the normal things you do to keep your life from completely unraveling. Everything just piles up and I look at it and can’t seem to start.\n\nIt got bad enough that I ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts. So… yes, it’s definitely affecting day-to-day functioning.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I’ve felt it quite like this before. I’ve had hard stretches, especially with everything around the divorce, but what made me come in now is that I just can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build after the divorce has collapsed, and I’m just sitting under it.\n\nSo… before this past month, I don’t know how to answer cleanly. I don’t want to say yes if it wasn’t the same, but this current feeling is what feels so overwhelming and stuck.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m kind of circling, but… before this past month, I don’t really know how to compare it cleanly.\n\nWhat made me come in now is that I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath it. So yes, the financial stuff is part of it, definitely, but it feels more like it opened up this whole sense that everything is ruined and I don’t know how to get out from under it.\n\nDoctor: Has this heavy, hopeless feeling been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been there pretty much every day. I mean, I wish I could say it comes and goes in a normal way, like I have a bad afternoon and then I reset, but it hasn’t felt like that.\n\nIt’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I just can’t shake, like everything I built back up after the divorce has collapsed and I’m just sitting under it. Even when I’m trying to do regular things or tell myself to keep moving, it’s still there in the background, like a weight pressing on everything. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been… it’s been more than just feeling bad and still getting through things. I’m not functioning the way I normally do.\n\nAt work, it got really obvious. I’m usually careful — I mean, my whole job depends on being careful and thinking through consequences — but I started making questionable calls, not like intentionally reckless, but I just wasn’t tracking things the way I should. I’d sit in meetings and kind of fall apart, either getting overwhelmed or not being able to explain myself clearly, and I could tell people were noticing. My supervisor actually had to take me off active project decisions for now, which was humiliating, but also probably necessary.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like the normal things you do to keep your life from completely unraveling. It’s like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Sleep has been… really bad lately.

Some nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then I just lie there staring at the ceiling, and my mind won’t stop. I keep replaying bills, what I still owe, things I might have messed up at work, conversations I think I handled wrong… it just loops. I’ll tell myself to stop thinking, but then I’m still awake, watching the room get lighter.

And other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and it doesn’t feel like I actually slept. It’s like I was unconscious for a while but not rested, if that makes sense. I wake up already tense, like my brain started the day before I did.

The money problems after the divorce were like… the thing that made me look around and realize how much had fallen apart. But the feeling itself is bigger than just bills or numbers.\n\nIt’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I can’t shake, like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has collapsed anyway. Like I did all this work to get some kind of stability again, and now I’m just sitting under the wreckage of it. So yes, the financial stuff is part of it, definitely, but it feels more like it opened up this whole sense that everything is ruined and I don’t know how to get out from under it.\n\nDoctor: Has this heavy, hopeless feeling been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been there pretty much every day. I mean, I wish I could say it comes and goes in a normal way, like I have a bad afternoon and then I reset, but it hasn’t felt like that.\n\nIt’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I just can’t shake, like everything I built back up after the divorce has collapsed and I’m just sitting under it. Even when I’m trying to do regular things or tell myself to keep moving, it’s still there in the background, like a weight pressing on everything. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been… it’s been more than just feeling bad and still getting through things. I’m not functioning the way I normally do.\n\nAt work, it got really obvious. I’m usually careful — I mean, my whole job depends on being careful and thinking through consequences — but I started making questionable calls, not like intentionally reckless, but I just wasn’t tracking things the way I should. I’d sit in meetings and kind of fall apart, either getting overwhelmed or not being able to explain myself clearly, and I could tell people were noticing. My supervisor actually had to take me off active project decisions for now, which was humiliating, but also probably necessary.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like the normal things you do to keep your life from completely unraveling. Everything just piles up and I look at it and can’t seem to start.\n\nIt got bad enough that I ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts. So… yes, it’s definitely affecting day-to-day functioning.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I’ve felt it quite like this before. I’ve had hard stretches, especially with everything around the divorce, but what made me come in now is that I just can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build after the divorce has collapsed, and I’m just sitting under it.\n\nSo… before this past month, I don’t know how to answer cleanly. Yes… I mean, not exactly like I had some detailed plan I was walking around with, but yes, I’ve been having thoughts that scare me.

It’s been more like… I’ll wake up, or I’ll be lying there not sleeping, and this thought comes in that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. It’s hard to say out loud because I don’t want to sound dramatic, but yes, those thoughts have been there.

I don’t want to say yes if it wasn’t the same, but this current feeling is what feels so overwhelming and stuck.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m kind of circling, but… before this past month, I don’t really know how to compare it cleanly.\n\nWhat made me come in now is that I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath it. That’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nI’ve had painful times before, and I don’t want to pretend I’ve never been sad or overwhelmed, but this… this feels stuck in a way I can’t seem to get out from under. So if you’re asking whether there was another clear two-week period exactly like this before now, I’m not sure I can honestly say yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really been paying attention to enjoyment as a separate thing, if that makes sense. A lot of things have just felt harder to do, so I’m not sure whether I’ve lost interest in them or whether I just don’t have the energy or focus to start.\n\nThere are things I normally like, but lately I don’t really reach for them in the same way. But I’m hesitating because I don’t want to overstate it or give you a clean yes if I’m not actually sure. I’ll tell myself to stop thinking, but then I’m still awake, watching the room get lighter.\n\nAnd other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and it doesn’t feel like I actually slept. It’s like I was unconscious for a while but not rested, if that makes sense. I wake up already tense, like my brain started the day before I did.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked “before this past month,” and I’m sorry, I keep getting stuck on the wording because I don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nWhat I can say clearly is that right now, the reason I came in is because I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. Like I can see the pieces of my life around me, but I can’t get out from under the weight of it.\n\nBefore now, I’ve had hard times, definitely. Nothing is coming to mind that feels as clear or as stuck as this does.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about killing yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not exactly like I had some detailed plan I was walking around with, but yes, I’ve been having thoughts that scare me.\n\nIt’s been more like… I’ll wake up, or I’ll be lying there not sleeping, and this thought comes in that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. I’m honestly not sure. I know I’ve had periods in my life where I was really down and not myself, but I’m having trouble separating what was just stress or being overwhelmed from what you’re asking—like depressed nearly every day for two full weeks and actually interfering with my functioning.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think they do. I know the financial problems after the divorce are real, and I don’t want to act like they’re nothing, because they’re not. They’ve been stressful and humiliating and scary in their own way.

But this feels bigger than that. It’s like I came in because I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling, and it’s not just “I’m worried about money” or “I’m upset about the divorce.” It feels like everything I tried to build back up afterward has collapsed, and I’m just sitting under it. Like I can understand, logically, why I’d be stressed—but the feeling itself is so much heavier than what I would expect from the situation.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just… I can’t seem to get out from under this heaviness. It’s like this hopeless feeling that sits on me all day, and I keep thinking about everything I worked so hard to rebuild after the divorce, and it feels like it’s all collapsed. Like I’m just sitting underneath it, looking at the pieces, and I don’t know how to move or what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, maybe, but it doesn’t feel like a bad day or a rough week. It feels bigger than that. Heavy. Kind of like there’s no air in the room, even when everything around me is technically still there. I just feel like I can’t shake it.\n\nDoctor: Did this heaviness start after the divorce?\n\nPatient: Not exactly right after the divorce itself, no. The divorce was… obviously it was a lot, and there was plenty of stress around it, but this particular heaviness really settled in about a month ago.\n\nIt kind of started around when the financial mess after the divorce became impossible to ignore — like all the loose ends and bills and numbers and obligations just caught up with me. That’s when it went from feeling stressed or overwhelmed to this constant cloud over everything. It’s been hanging over me for about a month now, pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Does this heaviness feel tied specifically to the financial problems after the divorce?\n\nPatient: It’s tied to it, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only that, if that makes sense. The money problems after the divorce were like… the thing that made me look around and realize how much had fallen apart. But the feeling itself is bigger than just bills or numbers.\n\nIt’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I can’t shake, like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has collapsed anyway. Like I did all this work to get some kind of stability again, and now I’m just sitting under the wreckage of it. It’s been there pretty much every day. I mean, I wish I could say it comes and goes in a normal way, like I have a bad afternoon and then I reset, but it hasn’t felt like that.\n\nIt’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I just can’t shake, like everything I built back up after the divorce has collapsed and I’m just sitting under it. Even when I’m trying to do regular things or tell myself to keep moving, it’s still there in the background, like a weight pressing on everything. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been… it’s been more than just feeling bad and still getting through things. I’m not functioning the way I normally do.\n\nAt work, it got really obvious. I’m usually careful — I mean, my whole job depends on being careful and thinking through consequences — but I started making questionable calls, not like intentionally reckless, but I just wasn’t tracking things the way I should. I’d sit in meetings and kind of fall apart, either getting overwhelmed or not being able to explain myself clearly, and I could tell people were noticing. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like the normal things you do to keep your life from completely unraveling. So… yes, it’s definitely affecting day-to-day functioning.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I’ve felt it quite like this before. I’ve had hard stretches, especially with everything around the divorce, but what made me come in now is that I just can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build after the divorce has collapsed, and I’m just sitting under it.\n\nSo… before this past month, I don’t know how to answer cleanly. It’s like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath it. That’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nI’ve had painful times before, and I don’t want to pretend I’ve never been sad or overwhelmed, but this… this feels stuck in a way I can’t seem to get out from under. So if you’re asking whether there was another clear two-week period exactly like this before now, I’m not sure I can honestly say yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really been paying attention to enjoyment as a separate thing, if that makes sense. A lot of things have just felt harder to do, so I’m not sure whether I’ve lost interest in them or whether I just don’t have the energy or focus to start.\n\nThere are things I normally like, but lately I don’t really reach for them in the same way. I don’t think I can say that for sure. So yes, the financial stuff is part of it, definitely, but it feels more like it opened up this whole sense that everything is ruined and I don’t know how to get out from under it.\n\nDoctor: Has this heavy, hopeless feeling been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been there pretty much every day. I mean, I wish I could say it comes and goes in a normal way, like I have a bad afternoon and then I reset, but it hasn’t felt like that.\n\nIt’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I just can’t shake, like everything I built back up after the divorce has collapsed and I’m just sitting under it. Even when I’m trying to do regular things or tell myself to keep moving, it’s still there in the background, like a weight pressing on everything. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been… it’s been more than just feeling bad and still getting through things. I’m not functioning the way I normally do.\n\nAt work, it got really obvious. I’m usually careful — I mean, my whole job depends on being careful and thinking through consequences — but I started making questionable calls, not like intentionally reckless, but I just wasn’t tracking things the way I should. I’d sit in meetings and kind of fall apart, either getting overwhelmed or not being able to explain myself clearly, and I could tell people were noticing. My supervisor actually had to take me off active project decisions for now, which was humiliating, but also probably necessary.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like the normal things you do to keep your life from completely unraveling. Everything just piles up and I look at it and can’t seem to start.\n\nIt got bad enough that I ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts. So… yes, it’s definitely affecting day-to-day functioning.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I’ve felt it quite like this before. I’ve had hard stretches, especially with everything around the divorce, but what made me come in now is that I just can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build after the divorce has collapsed, and I’m just sitting under it.\n\nSo… before this past month, I don’t know how to answer cleanly. I don’t want to say yes if it wasn’t the same, but this current feeling is what feels so overwhelming and stuck.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m kind of circling, but… before this past month, I don’t really know how to compare it cleanly.\n\nWhat made me come in now is that I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath it. That’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nI’ve had painful times before, and I don’t want to pretend I’ve never been sad or overwhelmed, but this… this feels stuck in a way I can’t seem to get out from under. I’ll tell myself to stop thinking, but then I’m still awake, watching the room get lighter.\n\nAnd other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and it doesn’t feel like I actually slept. That’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nI’ve had painful times before, and I don’t want to pretend I’ve never been sad or overwhelmed, but this… this feels stuck in a way I can’t seem to get out from under. So if you’re asking whether there was another clear two-week period exactly like this before now, I’m not sure I can honestly say yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really been paying attention to enjoyment as a separate thing, if that makes sense. A lot of things have just felt harder to do, so I’m not sure whether I’ve lost interest in them or whether I just don’t have the energy or focus to start.\n\nThere are things I normally like, but lately I don’t really reach for them in the same way. But I’m hesitating because I don’t want to overstate it or give you a clean yes if I’m not actually sure. It’s more like everything feels kind of distant, like I’m going through the motions or not even getting to the motions, rather than thinking, “Oh, I don’t enjoy that anymore.”\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I think maybe, but I’d need you to ask it a different way or give me examples.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… really bad lately.\n\nSome nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then I just lie there staring at the ceiling, and my mind won’t stop. I keep replaying bills, what I still owe, things I might have messed up at work, conversations I think I handled wrong… it just loops. I’ll tell myself to stop thinking, but then I’m still awake, watching the room get lighter.\n\nAnd other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and it doesn’t feel like I actually slept. It’s like I was unconscious for a while but not rested, if that makes sense. I wake up already tense, like my brain started the day before I did.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked “before this past month,” and I’m sorry, I keep getting stuck on the wording because I don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nWhat I can say clearly is that right now, the reason I came in is because I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s not just being sad for a day or having a rough week. It feels like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has collapsed, and I’m just… sitting underneath it. Like I can see the pieces of my life around me, but I can’t get out from under the weight of it.\n\nBefore now, I’ve had hard times, definitely. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. I’m trying to think back carefully, because I’ve had different appointments over the years where I talked about being stressed or overwhelmed, but I don’t remember ever being put on an antidepressant specifically for depression before this past month.

I don’t want to say “never” in a way that sounds careless, because medication names and reasons can get blurry over time, but if you mean, like, a doctor treated me for depression with an antidepressant—no, not that I can clearly remember. I don’t believe that happened.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.23, F43.21\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just… I can’t seem to get out from under this heaviness. It’s like this hopeless feeling that sits on me all day, and I keep thinking about everything I worked so hard to rebuild after the divorce, and it feels like it’s all collapsed. Like I’m just sitting underneath it, looking at the pieces, and I don’t know how to move or what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic, maybe, but it doesn’t feel like a bad day or a rough week. It feels bigger than that. Heavy. Kind of like there’s no air in the room, even when everything around me is technically still there. I just feel like I can’t shake it.\n\nDoctor: Did this heaviness start after the divorce?\n\nPatient: Not exactly right after the divorce itself, no. The divorce was… obviously it was a lot, and there was plenty of stress around it, but this particular heaviness really settled in about a month ago.\n\nIt kind of started around when the financial mess after the divorce became impossible to ignore — like all the loose ends and bills and numbers and obligations just caught up with me. That’s when it went from feeling stressed or overwhelmed to this constant cloud over everything. It’s been hanging over me for about a month now, pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Does this heaviness feel tied specifically to the financial problems after the divorce?\n\nPatient: It’s tied to it, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only that, if that makes sense. The money problems after the divorce were like… the thing that made me look around and realize how much had fallen apart. But the feeling itself is bigger than just bills or numbers.\n\nIt’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I can’t shake, like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has collapsed anyway. Like I did all this work to get some kind of stability again, and now I’m just sitting under the wreckage of it. So yes, the financial stuff is part of it, definitely, but it feels more like it opened up this whole sense that everything is ruined and I don’t know how to get out from under it.\n\nDoctor: Has this heavy, hopeless feeling been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been there pretty much every day. I mean, I wish I could say it comes and goes in a normal way, like I have a bad afternoon and then I reset, but it hasn’t felt like that.\n\nIt’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I just can’t shake, like everything I built back up after the divorce has collapsed and I’m just sitting under it. Even when I’m trying to do regular things or tell myself to keep moving, it’s still there in the background, like a weight pressing on everything. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been… it’s been more than just feeling bad and still getting through things. I’m not functioning the way I normally do.\n\nAt work, it got really obvious. I’m usually careful — I mean, my whole job depends on being careful and thinking through consequences — but I started making questionable calls, not like intentionally reckless, but I just wasn’t tracking things the way I should. I’d sit in meetings and kind of fall apart, either getting overwhelmed or not being able to explain myself clearly, and I could tell people were noticing. My supervisor actually had to take me off active project decisions for now, which was humiliating, but also probably necessary.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like the normal things you do to keep your life from completely unraveling. Everything just piles up and I look at it and can’t seem to start.\n\nIt got bad enough that I ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts. So… yes, it’s definitely affecting day-to-day functioning.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I’ve felt it quite like this before. I’ve had hard stretches, especially with everything around the divorce, but what made me come in now is that I just can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build after the divorce has collapsed, and I’m just sitting under it.\n\nSo… before this past month, I don’t know how to answer cleanly. I don’t want to say yes if it wasn’t the same, but this current feeling is what feels so overwhelming and stuck.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m kind of circling, but… before this past month, I don’t really know how to compare it cleanly.\n\nWhat made me come in now is that I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath it. That’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nI’ve had painful times before, and I don’t want to pretend I’ve never been sad or overwhelmed, but this… this feels stuck in a way I can’t seem to get out from under. So if you’re asking whether there was another clear two-week period exactly like this before now, I’m not sure I can honestly say yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really been paying attention to enjoyment as a separate thing, if that makes sense. A lot of things have just felt harder to do, so I’m not sure whether I’ve lost interest in them or whether I just don’t have the energy or focus to start.\n\nThere are things I normally like, but lately I don’t really reach for them in the same way. But I’m hesitating because I don’t want to overstate it or give you a clean yes if I’m not actually sure. It’s more like everything feels kind of distant, like I’m going through the motions or not even getting to the motions, rather than thinking, “Oh, I don’t enjoy that anymore.”\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I think maybe, but I’d need you to ask it a different way or give me examples.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… really bad lately.\n\nSome nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then I just lie there staring at the ceiling, and my mind won’t stop. I keep replaying bills, what I still owe, things I might have messed up at work, conversations I think I handled wrong… it just loops. I’ll tell myself to stop thinking, but then I’m still awake, watching the room get lighter.\n\nAnd other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and it doesn’t feel like I actually slept. It’s like I was unconscious for a while but not rested, if that makes sense. I wake up already tense, like my brain started the day before I did.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked “before this past month,” and I’m sorry, I keep getting stuck on the wording because I don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nWhat I can say clearly is that right now, the reason I came in is because I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s not just being sad for a day or having a rough week. It feels like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has collapsed, and I’m just… sitting underneath it. Like I can see the pieces of my life around me, but I can’t get out from under the weight of it.\n\nBefore now, I’ve had hard times, definitely. But whether there was another separate period before this month where I was depressed nearly every day for two weeks or more… I’m honestly not sure. Nothing is coming to mind that feels as clear or as stuck as this does.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about killing yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not exactly like I had some detailed plan I was walking around with, but yes, I’ve been having thoughts that scare me.\n\nIt’s been more like… I’ll wake up, or I’ll be lying there not sleeping, and this thought comes in that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to face another day, not have to keep trying to hold everything together. And at first I tried to tell myself it was just a passing thought, like I didn’t really mean it, but it started happening enough—and feeling intense enough—that I got scared of myself, honestly.\n\nThat’s part of why I reached out for help. I called the employee assistance program because I didn’t know what else to do in that moment, and I felt like if I kept it to myself it might get worse. It’s hard to say out loud because I don’t want to sound dramatic, but yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks and it was bad enough to interfere with your functioning?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I know I’ve had periods in my life where I was really down and not myself, but I’m having trouble separating what was just stress or being overwhelmed from what you’re asking—like depressed nearly every day for two full weeks and actually interfering with my functioning.\n\nNothing before this past month is coming to mind as clearly as this. I don’t want to say no if I’m forgetting something, but I also don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had hard stretches before. I guess the most honest answer is I don’t know, not in a way I can clearly identify right now.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem much stronger than you would expect from the financial problems after the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they do. I know the financial problems after the divorce are real, and I don’t want to act like they’re nothing, because they’re not. They’ve been stressful and humiliating and scary in their own way.\n\nBut this feels bigger than that. It’s like I came in because I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling, and it’s not just “I’m worried about money” or “I’m upset about the divorce.” It feels like everything I tried to build back up afterward has collapsed, and I’m just sitting under it. I don’t remember it being like this before.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble concentrating?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Diminished_Thinking\nmanifestation: \"I’ll look at a basic drainage calculation or a spreadsheet I’ve used for years and my mind just blanks, so I sit there rereading the same numbers without trusting any decision.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much, actually. That’s been one of the scariest parts because it’s showing up at work in ways I can’t really hide from myself.

I’ll be looking at something basic, like a drainage calculation or a spreadsheet I’ve used for years, and my mind just… blanks. Not like I don’t know how to do my job, exactly, but like the connection between seeing the numbers and trusting what they mean just isn’t there. I’ll reread the same line or the same set of values over and over, and I still don’t feel confident making even a normal decision from it.

And these are things I’ve done so many times before. That’s what makes it upsetting. I’ll sit there staring at the screen, going back through the same numbers again, checking and rechecking, and it feels like my brain won’t move forward. It’s not just being distracted for a minute—it’s like I can’t hold the thought long enough to use it.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just… I can’t seem to get out from under this heaviness. It’s been there pretty much every day. Even when I’m trying to do regular things or tell myself to keep moving, it’s still there in the background, like a weight pressing on everything. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been… it’s been more than just feeling bad and still getting through things. I’m not functioning the way I normally do.\n\nAt work, it got really obvious. I’m usually careful — I mean, my whole job depends on being careful and thinking through consequences — but I started making questionable calls, not like intentionally reckless, but I just wasn’t tracking things the way I should. I’d sit in meetings and kind of fall apart, either getting overwhelmed or not being able to explain myself clearly, and I could tell people were noticing. My supervisor actually had to take me off active project decisions for now, which was humiliating, but also probably necessary.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like the normal things you do to keep your life from completely unraveling. So… yes, it’s definitely affecting day-to-day functioning.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I’ve felt it quite like this before. I’ve had hard stretches, especially with everything around the divorce, but what made me come in now is that I just can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build after the divorce has collapsed, and I’m just sitting under it.\n\nSo… before this past month, I don’t know how to answer cleanly. I don’t want to say yes if it wasn’t the same, but this current feeling is what feels so overwhelming and stuck.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m kind of circling, but… before this past month, I don’t really know how to compare it cleanly.\n\nWhat made me come in now is that I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath it. That’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nI’ve had painful times before, and I don’t want to pretend I’ve never been sad or overwhelmed, but this… this feels stuck in a way I can’t seem to get out from under. So if you’re asking whether there was another clear two-week period exactly like this before now, I’m not sure I can honestly say yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really been paying attention to enjoyment as a separate thing, if that makes sense. A lot of things have just felt harder to do, so I’m not sure whether I’ve lost interest in them or whether I just don’t have the energy or focus to start.\n\nThere are things I normally like, but lately I don’t really reach for them in the same way. But I’m hesitating because I don’t want to overstate it or give you a clean yes if I’m not actually sure. It’s more like everything feels kind of distant, like I’m going through the motions or not even getting to the motions, rather than thinking, “Oh, I don’t enjoy that anymore.”\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I think maybe, but I’d need you to ask it a different way or give me examples.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… really bad lately.\n\nSome nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then I just lie there staring at the ceiling, and my mind won’t stop. I keep replaying bills, what I still owe, things I might have messed up at work, conversations I think I handled wrong… it just loops. I’ll tell myself to stop thinking, but then I’m still awake, watching the room get lighter.\n\nAnd other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and it doesn’t feel like I actually slept. It’s like I was unconscious for a while but not rested, if that makes sense. I wake up already tense, like my brain started the day before I did.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked “before this past month,” and I’m sorry, I keep getting stuck on the wording because I don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nWhat I can say clearly is that right now, the reason I came in is because I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s not just being sad for a day or having a rough week. It feels like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has collapsed, and I’m just… sitting underneath it. Like I can see the pieces of my life around me, but I can’t get out from under the weight of it.\n\nBefore now, I’ve had hard times, definitely. But whether there was another separate period before this month where I was depressed nearly every day for two weeks or more… I’m honestly not sure. Nothing is coming to mind that feels as clear or as stuck as this does.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about killing yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not exactly like I had some detailed plan I was walking around with, but yes, I’ve been having thoughts that scare me.\n\nIt’s been more like… I’ll wake up, or I’ll be lying there not sleeping, and this thought comes in that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to face another day, not have to keep trying to hold everything together. And at first I tried to tell myself it was just a passing thought, like I didn’t really mean it, but it started happening enough—and feeling intense enough—that I got scared of myself, honestly.\n\nThat’s part of why I reached out for help. I called the employee assistance program because I didn’t know what else to do in that moment, and I felt like if I kept it to myself it might get worse. It’s hard to say out loud because I don’t want to sound dramatic, but yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks and it was bad enough to interfere with your functioning?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I know I’ve had periods in my life where I was really down and not myself, but I’m having trouble separating what was just stress or being overwhelmed from what you’re asking—like depressed nearly every day for two full weeks and actually interfering with my functioning.\n\nNothing before this past month is coming to mind as clearly as this. I don’t want to say no if I’m forgetting something, but I also don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had hard stretches before. I guess the most honest answer is I don’t know, not in a way I can clearly identify right now.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem much stronger than you would expect from the financial problems after the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they do. I know the financial problems after the divorce are real, and I don’t want to act like they’re nothing, because they’re not. They’ve been stressful and humiliating and scary in their own way.\n\nBut this feels bigger than that. It’s like I came in because I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling, and it’s not just “I’m worried about money” or “I’m upset about the divorce.” It feels like everything I tried to build back up afterward has collapsed, and I’m just sitting under it. Like I can understand, logically, why I’d be stressed—but the feeling itself is so much heavier than what I would expect from the situation.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, did you ever have a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks and you knew for sure it was not just normal stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that for sure. I mean, I’ve definitely had times before where I was unhappy or worn down or felt really discouraged, but when you say depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks and knowing it wasn’t just normal stress… I don’t know that I can clearly point to a time like that.\n\nPart of the problem is I tend to explain things away while they’re happening. Like I’ll tell myself, “Well, anyone would feel bad under these circumstances,” or “You’re just tired,” or “This is just a rough patch.” So looking back, it’s hard for me to know what was something more serious versus what was me reacting to life being difficult.\n\nThis past month feels different in how clear and scary it’s been, but before that… I’m not comfortable saying yes with certainty. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: At any time before this past month, were you ever diagnosed with depression by a doctor or therapist?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so, but I’m trying to be careful with how I answer because I’ve talked to people before—like therapists or doctors at different points—but I don’t remember anyone clearly saying, “You have depression,” as an actual diagnosis.\n\nI may have described feeling low or overwhelmed in the past, and maybe someone used words like stress or talked about coping, but if you’re asking whether I was formally diagnosed with depression before this past month, I don’t have a clear memory of that happening. I don’t want to accidentally say yes just because the word came up in conversation at some point. So… no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you were treated with antidepressant medication for depression?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I’m trying to think back carefully, because I’ve had different appointments over the years where I talked about being stressed or overwhelmed, but I don’t remember ever being put on an antidepressant specifically for depression before this past month.\n\nI don’t want to say “never” in a way that sounds careless, because medication names and reasons can get blurry over time, but if you mean, like, a doctor treated me for depression with an antidepressant—no, not that I can clearly remember. I don’t believe that happened.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt this same kind of heavy, hopeless depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this past month? I… I don’t think it was this same kind. Like I can understand, logically, why I’d be stressed—but the feeling itself is so much heavier than what I would expect from the situation.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, did you ever have a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks and you knew for sure it was not just normal stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that for sure. I mean, I’ve definitely had times before where I was unhappy or worn down or felt really discouraged, but when you say depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks and knowing it wasn’t just normal stress… I don’t know that I can clearly point to a time like that.\n\nPart of the problem is I tend to explain things away while they’re happening. Like I’ll tell myself, “Well, anyone would feel bad under these circumstances,” or “You’re just tired,” or “This is just a rough patch.” So looking back, it’s hard for me to know what was something more serious versus what was me reacting to life being difficult.\n\nThis past month feels different in how clear and scary it’s been, but before that… I’m not comfortable saying yes with certainty. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: At any time before this past month, were you ever diagnosed with depression by a doctor or therapist?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so, but I’m trying to be careful with how I answer because I’ve talked to people before—like therapists or doctors at different points—but I don’t remember anyone clearly saying, “You have depression,” as an actual diagnosis.\n\nI may have described feeling low or overwhelmed in the past, and maybe someone used words like stress or talked about coping, but if you’re asking whether I was formally diagnosed with depression before this past month, I don’t have a clear memory of that happening. I don’t want to accidentally say yes just because the word came up in conversation at some point. So… no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you were treated with antidepressant medication for depression?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. It’s not just being sad or stressed. It feels like everything I tried to rebuild after the divorce has just collapsed all at once, and I’m sitting underneath it, and I can’t see how to get out from under it. I don’t remember it being like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. That’s been one of the scariest parts because it’s showing up at work in ways I can’t really hide from myself.\n\nI’ll be looking at something basic, like a drainage calculation or a spreadsheet I’ve used for years, and my mind just… blanks. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… yes, a lot.

It’s been this constant voice in my head telling me I’m just failing at everything. At work, I keep thinking I’m a terrible engineer, like I have no business being responsible for anything important. If I miss something small in a calculation, or even think I might have missed something, I’ll replay it over and over like it proves I’m incompetent. Same with emails—if I send something tense, or if someone replies in a way that feels even slightly cold, I’ll sit there thinking, “See, you ruin things. You make everything harder.”

And it’s not just work. I keep thinking I failed as a wife, too. Like the divorce is this evidence that I’m fundamentally hard to live with or not worth staying with. I know there are probably more complicated explanations, but lately my mind doesn’t really let me believe them. It just keeps coming back to me being the common problem.

I also feel like a burden. Even when people are trying to help, I feel guilty for needing anything from them. I keep thinking everyone would be better off if I could just get myself together and stop dragging all of this into the room. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, but yes, I’ve been feeling worthless.

So… yes, it’s definitely affecting day-to-day functioning.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I’ve felt it quite like this before. I’ve had hard stretches, especially with everything around the divorce, but what made me come in now is that I just can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build after the divorce has collapsed, and I’m just sitting under it.\n\nSo… before this past month, I don’t know how to answer cleanly. I don’t want to say yes if it wasn’t the same, but this current feeling is what feels so overwhelming and stuck.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m kind of circling, but… before this past month, I don’t really know how to compare it cleanly.\n\nWhat made me come in now is that I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath it. That’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nI’ve had painful times before, and I don’t want to pretend I’ve never been sad or overwhelmed, but this… this feels stuck in a way I can’t seem to get out from under. So if you’re asking whether there was another clear two-week period exactly like this before now, I’m not sure I can honestly say yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really been paying attention to enjoyment as a separate thing, if that makes sense. A lot of things have just felt harder to do, so I’m not sure whether I’ve lost interest in them or whether I just don’t have the energy or focus to start.\n\nThere are things I normally like, but lately I don’t really reach for them in the same way. But I’m hesitating because I don’t want to overstate it or give you a clean yes if I’m not actually sure. It’s more like everything feels kind of distant, like I’m going through the motions or not even getting to the motions, rather than thinking, “Oh, I don’t enjoy that anymore.”\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I think maybe, but I’d need you to ask it a different way or give me examples.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… really bad lately.\n\nSome nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then I just lie there staring at the ceiling, and my mind won’t stop. I keep replaying bills, what I still owe, things I might have messed up at work, conversations I think I handled wrong… it just loops. I’ll tell myself to stop thinking, but then I’m still awake, watching the room get lighter.\n\nAnd other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and it doesn’t feel like I actually slept. It’s like I was unconscious for a while but not rested, if that makes sense. I wake up already tense, like my brain started the day before I did.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked “before this past month,” and I’m sorry, I keep getting stuck on the wording because I don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nWhat I can say clearly is that right now, the reason I came in is because I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s not just being sad for a day or having a rough week. It feels like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has collapsed, and I’m just… sitting underneath it. Like I can see the pieces of my life around me, but I can’t get out from under the weight of it.\n\nBefore now, I’ve had hard times, definitely. But whether there was another separate period before this month where I was depressed nearly every day for two weeks or more… I’m honestly not sure. Nothing is coming to mind that feels as clear or as stuck as this does.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about killing yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not exactly like I had some detailed plan I was walking around with, but yes, I’ve been having thoughts that scare me.\n\nIt’s been more like… I’ll wake up, or I’ll be lying there not sleeping, and this thought comes in that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to face another day, not have to keep trying to hold everything together. And at first I tried to tell myself it was just a passing thought, like I didn’t really mean it, but it started happening enough—and feeling intense enough—that I got scared of myself, honestly.\n\nThat’s part of why I reached out for help. I called the employee assistance program because I didn’t know what else to do in that moment, and I felt like if I kept it to myself it might get worse. It’s hard to say out loud because I don’t want to sound dramatic, but yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks and it was bad enough to interfere with your functioning?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I know I’ve had periods in my life where I was really down and not myself, but I’m having trouble separating what was just stress or being overwhelmed from what you’re asking—like depressed nearly every day for two full weeks and actually interfering with my functioning.\n\nNothing before this past month is coming to mind as clearly as this. I don’t want to say no if I’m forgetting something, but I also don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had hard stretches before. Like I can understand, logically, why I’d be stressed—but the feeling itself is so much heavier than what I would expect from the situation.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, did you ever have a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks and you knew for sure it was not just normal stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that for sure. Like I’ll tell myself, “Well, anyone would feel bad under these circumstances,” or “You’re just tired,” or “This is just a rough patch.” So looking back, it’s hard for me to know what was something more serious versus what was me reacting to life being difficult.\n\nThis past month feels different in how clear and scary it’s been, but before that… I’m not comfortable saying yes with certainty. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: At any time before this past month, were you ever diagnosed with depression by a doctor or therapist?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so, but I’m trying to be careful with how I answer because I’ve talked to people before—like therapists or doctors at different points—but I don’t remember anyone clearly saying, “You have depression,” as an actual diagnosis.\n\nI may have described feeling low or overwhelmed in the past, and maybe someone used words like stress or talked about coping, but if you’re asking whether I was formally diagnosed with depression before this past month, I don’t have a clear memory of that happening. I don’t want to accidentally say yes just because the word came up in conversation at some point. So… no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you were treated with antidepressant medication for depression?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I’m trying to think back carefully, because I’ve had different appointments over the years where I talked about being stressed or overwhelmed, but I don’t remember ever being put on an antidepressant specifically for depression before this past month.\n\nI don’t want to say “never” in a way that sounds careless, because medication names and reasons can get blurry over time, but if you mean, like, a doctor treated me for depression with an antidepressant—no, not that I can clearly remember. It’s not just being sad or stressed. That’s been the clearest this past month.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether I had this exact kind of heavy, hopeless depression nearly every day for two weeks before now… I don’t know that I can say yes. I don’t remember it being like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. That’s been one of the scariest parts because it’s showing up at work in ways I can’t really hide from myself.\n\nI’ll be looking at something basic, like a drainage calculation or a spreadsheet I’ve used for years, and my mind just… blanks. Not like I don’t know how to do my job, exactly, but like the connection between seeing the numbers and trusting what they mean just isn’t there. I’ll reread the same line or the same set of values over and over, and I still don’t feel confident making even a normal decision from it.\n\nAnd these are things I’ve done so many times before. That’s what makes it upsetting. I’ll sit there staring at the screen, going back through the same numbers again, checking and rechecking, and it feels like my brain won’t move forward. It’s not just being distracted for a minute—it’s like I can’t hold the thought long enough to use it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has gone down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear drop in appetite in the way I think you mean, like suddenly not eating at all or food seeming disgusting.\n\nIt’s been kind of hard for me to separate appetite from just… how disorganized and weighed down I feel day to day. Some meals I don’t really care about, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m less hungry or because I’m just not paying attention to it. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed my appetite going down lately, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, a lot.\n\nIt’s been this constant voice in my head telling me I’m just failing at everything. At work, I keep thinking I’m a terrible engineer, like I have no business being responsible for anything important. If I miss something small in a calculation, or even think I might have missed something, I’ll replay it over and over like it proves I’m incompetent. Same with emails—if I send something tense, or if someone replies in a way that feels even slightly cold, I’ll sit there thinking, “See, you ruin things. You make everything harder.”\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I keep thinking I failed as a wife, too. Like the divorce is this evidence that I’m fundamentally hard to live with or not worth staying with. I know there are probably more complicated explanations, but lately my mind doesn’t really let me believe them. It just keeps coming back to me being the common problem.\n\nI also feel like a burden. Even when people are trying to help, I feel guilty for needing anything from them. I keep thinking everyone would be better off if I could just get myself together and stop dragging all of this into the room. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, but yes, I’ve been feeling worthless."
